Contract-based Design and Verification of Multi-Agent Systems with Quantitative Temporal Requirements
Rafael Dewes, Rayna Dimitrova

Abstract
Quantitative requirements play an important role in the context of multi-agent systems, where there is often a trade-off between the tasks of individual agents and the constraints that the agents must jointly adhere to. We study multi-agent systems whose requirements are formally specified in the quantitative temporal logic LTL[ ℱ \mathcal{F} ] as a combination of local task specifications for the individual agents and a shared safety constraint. The intricate dependencies between the individual agents entailed by their local and shared objectives make the design of multi-agent systems error-prone, and their verification time-consuming. In this paper we address this problem by proposing a novel notion of quantitative assume-guarantee contracts, that enables the compositional design and verification of multi-agent systems with quantitative temporal specifications. The crux of these contracts lies in their ability to capture the coordination between the individual agents to achieve an optimal value of the overall specification under any possible behavior of the external environment. We show that the proposed framework improves the scalability and modularity of formal verification of multi-agent systems against quantitative temporal specifications.



CISPA Helmholtz Center for Information Security

{rafael.dewes, dimitrova}@cispa.de

1
Introduction

Multi-agent systems (MAS) have become increasingly important in various domains, such as robotics, distributed computing, and autonomous vehicles.
In MAS
(
Wooldridge 2009
)
, agents operate autonomously but may need to interact with each other to achieve shared objectives.
Compared to larger monolithic systems, the ability of multiple agents to work collaboratively towards a common goal offers significant advantages in terms of efficiency and flexibility.

Coordination in MAS is a major challenge, as it involves not only the synchronization of actions among agents but also handles interdependencies and emergent conflicts.
While it is crucial for aligning strategies, it can also introduce overhead and complexity, especially in dynamic and unpredictable environments.
Additionally, achieving shared goals often requires balancing individual agent preferences and global objectives, which can introduce conflict.

To address these challenges,
contract-based design

(
Benveniste et al. 2018
)
offers a structured way to define and manage interactions between agents.
Compositional design approaches employ different forms of contracts, such as assume-guarantee contracts
(
Benveniste et al. 2007
)
. However, traditional contract frameworks fall short in handling complex quantitative requirements and ensuring optimal collective performance,
where agents must adapt to varying conditions and still aim for the best possible outcomes.

In this paper, we tackle the problem of designing an expressive contract framework for the compositional and best-effort satisfaction of quantitative specifications in MAS with collective goals.
Our proposed framework extends traditional contract-based methods by incorporating quantitative measures and best-effort guarantees.
Agents must strive for best possible outcomes even when perfect satisfaction is not feasible,
ensuring that the system remains functional and effective in suboptimal environments.
The approach includes formal verification methods to establish that the decomposition of global specifications into local contracts maintains the integrity of overall system goals,
ensuring that individual agent behaviors align with the collective objectives.

We consider compositional specifications in the quantitative temporal logic LTL
[\mathcal{F}]

(
Almagor, Boker, and Kupferman 2016
)
,
which extends linear temporal logic (LTL) with quality operators.
For a given trace, an LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
formula evaluates to a value from a finite set of possible satisfaction values.
We focus on specifications combining requirements
local
to individual agents and
shared
requirements constraining the agents’ interaction.
Local requirements only include actions of the respective agent,
while shared objectives can contain any actions but must be safety properties.
Our framework ensures that individual agent behavior aligns with both local and shared system-wide objectives.
Existing work mostly studies the qualitative case where agents have a common objective.
In our model agents are cooperative, unlike non-cooperative MAS where concepts like Nash equilibria are central
(
Kupferman, Perelli, and Vardi 2016
)
.
Closest to our approach is
(
Dewes and Dimitrova 2023
)
, however our framework is more expressive and, unlike
(
Dewes and Dimitrova 2023
)
, our decomposition contracts are complete.
We will highlight these differences in
Section

4
.

Best-effort satisfaction means the highest satisfaction value enabled by the environment must be met by the system.
In contrast to previous work, our contracts take this into account to allow for different levels of coordination based on the inputs.
It relates to the qualitative notion of dominant strategies
(
Damm and Finkbeiner 2014
)
that perform as good as the best alternative.
We build on the idea of “good-enough” realizability
(
Almagor and Kupferman 2020
)

to encompass quantitative compositional specifications.

The following example illustrates how the best-effort interpretation of compositional quantitative specifications leads to conditional obligations in a MAS.

Example 1.1
.

In this example, we consider a MAS with three agents modelling vehicles: two of them (agents
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
) can transport goods to a construction site, and the third (agent
a_{3}
) provides a visual feed.
The desired behavior of the overall system is specified as an LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
formula

\phantom{aaaaa}\Phi=\frac{1}{6}\varphi^{1}_{\mathit{local}}\oplus\frac{1}{6}\varphi^{2}_{\mathit{local}}\oplus\frac{1}{6}\varphi^{3}_{\mathit{local}}\oplus\frac{1}{2}\Psi_{\mathit{shared}},

which is the weighted sum (
\oplus
) of the sub-formulas
\varphi^{i}_{\mathit{local}}
and
\psi_{\mathit{shared}}
.
Each agent
a_{i}
has a local specification
\varphi^{i}_{\mathit{local}}
that prescribes their individual objectives.
The shared requirement
\Psi_{\mathit{shared}}
encodes a task assigned collectively to all agents.
Here, the local requirements are of equal priority, while the shared requirement
\Psi_{\mathit{shared}}
is weighed higher.

The agents operate in an environment that can issue calls to move to the site and send weather warnings, modeled with propositions
\mathsf{call}
and
\mathsf{badw}
respectively.

\Psi_{\mathit{shared}}
requires that after each call,
a_{3}
and at least one of
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
should respond in one or two time steps.
According to
\varphi^{i}_{\mathit{local}}
, vehicles
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
cannot travel from the base to the site in an instant,
and it is undesirable to travel during bad weather.
We also require that each of
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
spends two consecutive time steps at the base infinitely often.
The vehicle modelled by
a_{3}
travels faster, and is unaffected by the weather, but it must be at the base infinitely often (to archive data).

Best-effort satisfaction of
\Phi
means that it must be satisfied as well as possible under the current environment, by the system as a whole.
Whenever
\mathsf{call}
and
\mathsf{badw}
occur together, to satisfy
\Psi_{\mathit{shared}}
either
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
must travel in bad weather, thus lowering the overall value of
\Phi
.
If the frequency of this is high enough, then either both
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
must travel in bad conditions,
or one of them will be unable to stay at the base for two steps, violating
\varphi^{i}_{\mathit{local}}
.

There is an obvious need for coordination between the agents to collectively good-enough satisfy
\Phi
.
Here, the agents must distribute the tasks to satisfy
\Psi_{\mathit{shared}}
and agree on values with which the local specifications for
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
are satisfied.
Existing contract notions are not suitable for expressing this balancing of quantitative obligations.
We present a framework for contract-based design that makes it possible to capture such coordination in the form of a contract.
This enables independent design of the individual agents, leaving flexibility for the implementation of each agent outside of their shared obligations.

2
Preliminaries

In this section we recall definitions and notation necessary for presenting our framework,
such as formal languages, the specification logic LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
, and the formalization of best-effort, or “good-enough”, satisfaction.

Languages

Let
\Sigma
be a finite alphabet.
The set of finite (infinite) words over
\Sigma
is denoted by
\Sigma^{*}
(resp.
\Sigma^{\omega}
).
For
\sigma=\sigma_{0}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\ldots\in\Sigma^{\omega}
,
we denote
\sigma[i]=\sigma_{i}
, and with
\sigma[i,\infty)=\sigma_{i},\sigma_{i+1},\ldots
the suffix of
\sigma
starting at position
i
.
For
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{*}
and
\sigma^{\prime\prime}\in\Sigma^{*}\cup\Sigma^{\omega}
, we denote with
\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\sigma^{\prime\prime}
their concatenation.
A language
L\subseteq\Sigma^{\omega}
is a
safety language
if and only if for every
\sigma\in\Sigma^{\omega}\setminus L
there exists a finite prefix
\sigma^{\prime}
of
\sigma
such that
\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\sigma^{\prime\prime}\not\in L
for every

\sigma^{\prime\prime}\in\Sigma^{\omega}
.

For a set
X
, we denote with
2^{X}
the powerset of
X
.
For a word
\sigma
over alphabet
2^{X}
and a subset
Y\subseteq X
we denote with
\mathsf{proj}(\sigma,Y)
the projection of
\sigma
onto the alphabet
2^{Y}
.
For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we define
[n]:=\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
Given disjoint sets
X_{1},\ldots,X_{m}
and
for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
a word
\sigma_{i}
over the alphabet
2^{X_{i}}
,
we define the parallel composition
\parallel_{i=1}^{m}\sigma_{i}
of
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{m}
such that
(\parallel_{i=1}^{m}\sigma_{i})[j]=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}\sigma_{i}[j]
for all
j
.

The Specification Language LTL
[\mathcal{F}]

We now recall the temporal logic LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
introduced in
(
Almagor, Boker, and Kupferman 2016
)
.
Let
\mathit{AP}
be a set of Boolean atomic propositions, and

\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{f:[0,1]^{k}\to[0,1]\mid k\in\mathbb{N}\}
a set of functions.
The LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
formulas are generated by the grammar

\begin{array}[]{c}\varphi::=p\mid\mathsf{true}\mid\mathsf{false}\mid f(\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{k})\mid\operatorname{\hbox to7.41pt{\vbox to7.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.70276pt\lower-1.11943pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 M 4.77 3.57 C 4.77 6.21 2.63 8.34 0 8.34 C -2.63 8.34 -4.77 6.21 -4.77 3.57 C -4.77 0.94 -2.63 -1.19 0 -1.19 C 2.63 -1.19 4.77 0.94 4.77 3.57 Z M 0 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\varphi\mid\varphi_{1}\LTLu\varphi_{2},\end{array}

where
p\in\mathit{AP}
is an atomic proposition, and
f\in\mathcal{F}
.

We consider sets
\mathcal{F}
that include functions representing the Boolean operators, i.e.,
\{f_{\neg},f_{\land},f_{\lor}\}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
, where
f_{\neg}(x):=1-x
,
f_{\land}(x,y):=\min\{x,y\}
and
f_{\vee}(x,y):=\max\{x,y\}
.
For ease of notation, we use the operators
\neg,\land,\lor
instead of the corresponding functions.
One useful function is
the weighted average
x\oplus_{\lambda}y:=\lambda\cdot x+(1-\lambda)\cdot y
, where
\lambda\in\{0,1\}
.
Abusing notation, we often use
\oplus
with more than two arguments.

\operatorname{\hbox to7.41pt{\vbox to7.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.70276pt\lower-1.11943pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 M 4.77 3.57 C 4.77 6.21 2.63 8.34 0 8.34 C -2.63 8.34 -4.77 6.21 -4.77 3.57 C -4.77 0.94 -2.63 -1.19 0 -1.19 C 2.63 -1.19 4.77 0.94 4.77 3.57 Z M 0 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
and
\LTLu
are the LTL
next
and
until
operators respectively
(
Baier and Katoen 2008
)
.
We define the usual derived temporal operators

finally

\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\varphi:=\mathsf{true}\LTLu\varphi
and

globally

\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\varphi:=\neg(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\neg\varphi)
.

\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}

requires its argument to hold at every step from now on;

\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
requires that it holds at some point in the future;

\operatorname{\hbox to7.41pt{\vbox to7.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.70276pt\lower-1.11943pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 M 4.77 3.57 C 4.77 6.21 2.63 8.34 0 8.34 C -2.63 8.34 -4.77 6.21 -4.77 3.57 C -4.77 0.94 -2.63 -1.19 0 -1.19 C 2.63 -1.19 4.77 0.94 4.77 3.57 Z M 0 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
requires it to be true in the next step.

The semantics of LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
is defined with respect to words in
(2^{AP})^{\omega}
, and maps an LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
formula
\varphi
and a word
\sigma\in(2^{AP})^{\omega}
, to a value
\llbracket\varphi,\sigma\rrbracket\in[0,1]
.
For
f\in\mathcal{F}
, we define
\llbracket f(\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{k}),\sigma\rrbracket:=f(\llbracket\varphi_{1},\sigma\rrbracket,\ldots,\llbracket\varphi_{k},\sigma\rrbracket)
.
The semantics of the
until
operator
\LTLu
is
\llbracket\varphi_{1}\LTLu\varphi_{2},\sigma\rrbracket:=\max_{i\geq 0}\{\min\{\llbracket\varphi_{2},\sigma[i,\infty)\rrbracket,\min_{0\leq j<i}\llbracket\varphi_{1},\sigma[j,\infty)\rrbracket\}\}.

We refer the reader to
(
Almagor, Boker, and Kupferman 2016
)
for the full definition of the semantics.
We denote with
\mathit{Vals}(\varphi):=\{\llbracket\varphi,\sigma\rrbracket\mid\sigma\in(2^{AP})^{\omega}\}
the set of possible values of an LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
formula
\varphi
.

(
Almagor, Boker, and Kupferman 2016
)
showed that
|\mathit{Vals}(\varphi)|\leq 2^{|\varphi|}
for every LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
formula
\varphi
, where
|\varphi|
is the formula’s description size.
Thus, each formula’s set of possible values is finite.

Example 2.1
.

We formalize
Example

1.1
using LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
.
The location and movement of the vehicles are modelled by propositions
\mathsf{at\_base}_{i}
and
\mathsf{at\_site}_{i}
.
For
\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{i}:=

\displaystyle\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}

3
Quantitative Temporal Specifications of Multi-Agent Systems

We now present a formal model of MAS and the notion of
compositional LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
specifications
of such systems introduced in
(
Dewes and Dimitrova 2023
)
.

Formal Model for Multi-Agent Systems

We consider MAS where agents interact both with each other and the external environment via Boolean variables, i.e., atomic propositions.
We fix a set
\mathit{AP}
of atomic propositions such that
\mathit{AP}=\mathit{I}\uplus\mathit{O}
, for a finite set
\mathit{I}
of
input atomic propositions
and
a finite set
\mathit{O}
of
output atomic propositions
that are disjoint, i.e.,
\mathit{I}\cap\mathit{O}=\emptyset
.
The input propositions
\mathit{I}
are updated by the external environment and the output propositions
\mathit{O}
are updated by the agents in the system.
We call words in
(2^{\mathit{I}\cup\mathit{O}})^{\omega}

(execution) traces
and those in
(2^{\mathit{I}})^{\omega}

input traces
.

An
agent
in the system is modelled as a Moore machine

M=(\mathit{I}_{M},\mathit{O}_{M},S,s^{\mathit{init}},\rho,\mathit{Out})
, where
\mathit{I}_{M}
and
\mathit{O}_{M}
are
M
’s sets of input and output propositions,
S
is a finite set of states, with initial state
s^{\mathit{init}}\in S
, transition function
\rho:S\times 2^{\mathit{I}_{M}}\to S
, and output labeling function
\mathit{Out}:S\to 2^{\mathit{O}_{M}}
.
For input trace
\sigma_{\mathit{I}_{M}}\in(2^{\mathit{I}_{M}})^{\omega}
,

M
produces the output trace

M(\sigma_{\mathit{I}_{M}})\in(2^{\mathit{O}_{M}})^{\omega}
such that

M(\sigma_{\mathit{I}_{M}})[i]=\mathit{Out}(s_{i})
,
where the sequence of states
s_{0}s_{1}\ldots\in S^{\omega}
is such that

s_{0}=s^{\mathit{init}}
, and
s_{i+1}=\rho(s_{i},\sigma_{\mathit{I}_{M}}[i])
for all
i
.

A
multi-agent system
(MAS) is a tuple
\mathcal{S}=(\mathit{I},\mathit{O},\mathcal{M})
, where
\mathit{I}
and
\mathit{O}
are the sets of input and output propositions respectively,

\mathcal{M}=\langle M_{1},\ldots M_{n}\rangle
is a tuple of Moore machines representing the agents, where

M_{a}=(\mathit{I}_{a},\mathit{O}_{a},S_{a},s_{a}^{init},\rho_{a},\mathit{Out}_{a})
is such that
(1) for every
a,a^{\prime}\in[n]
with
a\neq a^{\prime}
it holds that
\mathit{O}_{a}\cap\mathit{O}_{a^{\prime}}=\emptyset
, (2)
\biguplus_{a=1}^{n}\mathit{O}_{a}=\mathit{O}
, and (3)
\mathit{I}_{a}=\mathit{I}\cup(\mathit{O}\setminus\mathit{O}_{a})
.
Conditions (1) and (2) state that the sets of output propositions of the individual agents partition
\mathit{O}
. Condition (3) stipulates that each agent observes all input propositions
\mathit{I}
and all output propositions of the other agents.
We denote with
\mathsf{Agt}:=[n]
the set of all agents, and with
\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}=\bigcup_{a^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Agt}\setminus\{a\}}\mathit{O}_{a^{\prime}}
the set of outputs of agents different from
a
.
Our model uses a composition of Moore machines, similar to Moore synchronous game structures in
(
Alur, Henzinger, and Kupferman 2002
)
, however here each machine models the implementation of an agent.
Given an input trace
\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\in(2^{\mathit{I}})^{\omega}
, a MAS generates an output trace
\sigma_{\mathit{O}}\in(2^{\mathit{O}})^{\omega}
,
which we denote by
(\parallel_{c=1}^{n}M_{a})(\sigma_{\mathit{I}})
,
such that
\mathsf{proj}(\sigma_{\mathit{O}},\mathit{O}_{a})=M_{a}(\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\parallel\mathsf{proj}(\sigma_{\mathit{O}},\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}))
for all
a
.
That is,
(\parallel_{a=1}^{n}M_{a})(\sigma_{\mathit{I}})
is the
composition of all the output traces
of the agents in
\mathcal{S}
.
We define the set of traces
\mathcal{L}(M_{a})\subseteq(2^{\mathit{I}\cup\mathit{O}})^{\omega}
as
\mathcal{L}(M_{a}):=\{\sigma\in 2^{\mathit{I}\cup\mathit{O}})^{\omega}\mid M_{a}(\mathsf{proj}(\sigma,\mathit{I}_{a}))=\mathsf{proj}(\sigma,\mathit{O}_{a})\}
.

Good-Enough Satisfaction of LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
Specifications

Following
(
Almagor and Kupferman 2020
)
, we consider
good-enough satisfaction
of an LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
specification
\Phi
by a system
\mathcal{S}
. Intuitively, we require that for every input trace, the system’s output results in the best value for
\Phi
possible for this input trace.
To formalize this, we use the notion of
v
-hopeful input sequences

(
Almagor and Kupferman 2020
)
.
Those are the input sequences for which there exists output sequences such that
\Phi
has value
v
in the resulting executions.
Formally,
for
v\in[0,1]
,
the set of
v
-hopeful input sequences for the formula
\Phi
is

\mathsf{Hopeful}(v,\Phi):=\{\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\in(2^{\mathit{I}})^{\omega}\mid\exists\sigma_{\mathit{O}}\in(2^{\mathit{O}})^{\omega}.~\llbracket\Phi,(\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\parallel\sigma_{\mathit{O}})\rrbracket=v\}
.

Definition 1

(Good-Enough Satisfaction)
.

Given an LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
formula
\Phi
over
\mathit{AP}=\mathit{I}\uplus\mathit{O}
,
and a MAS
\mathcal{S}=(\mathit{I},\mathit{O},\mathcal{M})
,
we say that
\mathcal{S}
satisfies
\Phi
, denoted
\mathcal{M}\models\Phi
,
if and only if for every
v\in[0,1]
and every

v
-hopeful input sequence
\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\in\mathsf{Hopeful}(v,\Phi)
, it holds that

\llbracket\Phi,\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\parallel\mathcal{M}(\sigma_{\mathit{I}})\rrbracket\geq v
.

Compositional LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
Specifications

In this paper we focus on a particular class of LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
specifications for MAS, called
compositional specifications
, introduced in
(
Dewes and Dimitrova 2023
)
, which studies the problem of automatic synthesis of MAS from such specifications.
Compositional specifications are given as a combination of local specifications for the individual agents and a shared requirement.
Formally, a
compositional specification
is of the form

\Phi=\mathit{comb}(\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{1},\ldots,\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{n},\Psi_{\mathit{shared}})
where

(1)

\mathit{comb}:[0,1]^{n+1}\to[0,1]
is non-decreasing in each subset of arguments: if
\mathit{comb}(v^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,v^{\prime}_{n+1})<\mathit{comb}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n+1})
, then,
v_{i}^{\prime}<v_{i}
for some
i\in[n+1]
.

(2)

The
shared specification

\Psi_{\mathit{shared}}
is a
safety
LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
specification.
This means that
for every
\sigma\in(2^{\mathit{AP}})^{\omega}
and

v\in\mathit{Vals}(\Psi_{\mathit{shared}})
,
if
\llbracket\Psi_{\mathit{shared}},\sigma\rrbracket<v
,
then there exists a prefix
\sigma^{\prime}
of
\sigma
,
such that
\llbracket\Psi_{\mathit{shared}},\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\sigma^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket<v
for every infinite continuation
\sigma^{\prime\prime}\in(2^{\mathit{AP}})^{\omega}
of
\sigma^{\prime}
.

(3)

For each agent
a
, the
local specification

\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{a}
for agent
a
refers only to atomic propositions in
\mathit{O}_{a}\cup\mathit{I}
.

Such specifications occur in MAS where we have task specifications for individual agents describing their desired behavior, as well as a shared safety constraint.
Going forward, we write
\Phi
to mean a compositional specification
\Phi=\mathit{comb}(\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{1},\ldots,\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{n},\Psi_{\mathit{shared}})
.

4
Quantitative Assume-Guarantee Contracts

In this section we introduce a new notion of contracts for MAS with compositional LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
specifications as defined in
Section

3
.
These contracts, termed
good-enough decomposition contracts
(GEDC) can express coordination between the agents while taking into account the quantitative nature of LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
requirements.
In contrast to traditional contracts used for compositional design and verification, the type of contracts we propose allows the specification of the quantitative contribution of each agent’s local requirements to the satisfaction value of the overall specification.
This, in turn, allows for assumptions and guarantees that impose weaker restrictions on agents’ behavior by stating
what
the satisfaction values of the local specifications should be, and not
how
these values should be achieved.
After introducing GEDC, we define what it means for a MAS to satisfy a GEDC, and show that this notion is sound and complete.

A GEDC for a compositional LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
specification
\Phi
consists of two components.
The first, called
allowed decompositions map
, specifies for each possible value
v
of
\Phi
a set of
value decompositions
describing how the satisfaction of each local specification and the shared specification contribute to achieving value
v
for
\Phi
.
The second component of a GEDC associates with each value
v
an
assume-guarantee contract
that specifies, in terms of behaviors, how the agents cooperate to achieve the desired satisfaction values.

We begin with the definition of value decompositions.
Intuitively, the decompositions of
v
with respect to
\Phi=\mathit{comb}(\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{1},\ldots,\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{n},\Psi_{\mathit{shared}})
are tuples of values for
\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{n},\Psi_{\mathit{shared}}
such that their combination results in
v
.
Formally, given
v\in\mathit{Vals}(\Phi)
, a
value decomposition
of
v
is a vector

\mathbf{d}\in(\bigtimes_{a=1}^{n}\mathit{Vals}(\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{a}))\times\mathit{Vals}(\Psi_{\mathit{shared}})
where

\mathit{comb}(\mathbf{d})=v
.
If
\mathbf{d}=(\langle u_{a}\rangle_{a=1}^{n},w)
, we denote the components of
\mathbf{d}
by

\mathbf{d}[a]:=u_{a}
for
a\in[n]
, and
\mathbf{d}[s]:=w
.
For every
v\in\mathit{Vals}(\Phi)
, we denote with
\mathit{Dec}(\Phi,v)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
the set consisting of all possible value decompositions of
v
.

Next, we define the notion of
allowed decompositions map
for
\Phi
.
Intuitively, the set
\mathit{ADec}
associates with each
v\in\mathit{Vals}(\Phi)
the value decompositions allowed by the contract for
v
-hopeful input sequences.
To ensure generality,
\mathit{ADec}
allow different sets of decompositions to be associated with different subsets
H
of
\mathsf{Hopeful}(v,\Phi)
.

Definition 2
.

An
allowed decompositions map
for
\Phi
is a set
\mathit{ADec}\subseteq\mathit{Vals}(\Phi)\times 2^{((2^{\mathit{I}})^{\omega})}\times 2^{\bigcup_{v\in\mathit{Vals}(\Phi)}\mathit{Dec}(\Phi,v)}
where:

(i)

(Input coverage)
For every
v\in\mathit{Vals}(\Phi)
and
\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\in\mathsf{Hopeful}(v,\Phi)
, there exists
(v^{\prime},H,D)\in\mathit{ADec}
such that
v^{\prime}\geq v
and
\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\in H
.
This ensures that every
v
-hopeful input sequence is covered by some set
H
for value
v^{\prime}\geq v
.

(ii)

(Satisfiable decompositions)
For every
(v,H,D)\in\mathit{ADec}
,
D\subseteq\mathit{Dec}(\Phi,v)
and for every
\mathbf{d}\in D
there exists
\sigma\in(2^{\mathit{I}\cup\mathit{O}})^{\omega}

such that
\llbracket\Psi_{\mathit{shared}},\sigma\rrbracket=\mathbf{d}[s]
and
\llbracket\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{a},\sigma\rrbracket=\mathbf{d}[a]
for all
a\in[n]
.
That is,
D
only contains satisfiable decompositions from
\mathit{Dec}(\Phi,v)
.

For every input sequence
\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\in(2^{\mathit{I}})^{\omega}
and
v\in\mathit{Vals}(\Phi)
we define

\mathit{ADec}(v,\sigma_{\mathit{I}}):=\{\mathbf{d}\mid\exists(v,H,D)\in\mathit{ADec}.~\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\in H\text{ and }\mathbf{d}\in D\}

to be the set of decompositions of
v
allowed by
\mathit{ADec}
for the input sequence
\sigma_{\mathit{I}}
.
Since every input sequence
\sigma_{\mathit{I}}
is hopeful for some value in
\mathit{Vals}(\Phi)
,
condition
(i)
in
Definition

2
implies that

\mathit{ADec}(v,\sigma_{\mathit{I}})
is non-empty for some
v
.
This ensures that a system which conforms with

\mathit{ADec}
has appropriate obligations w.r.t. every input sequence.
Furthermore, condition
(ii)
guarantees that these obligations are met by satisfiable value decompositons.

For a given specification
\Phi
, there may be multiple possible allowed decompositions maps that differ in the obligations they impose on the individual agents.
Next, we show the possible value decompositions for the formula in
Example

1.1
and one possible allowed decompositions map.

Example 4.1
.

The worst-case satisfaction value for
\Phi
in
Example

1.1
is
\frac{5}{6}
.
If
\mathsf{badw}
and
\mathsf{call}
are constantly true, this is the best that can be achieved:

\Psi_{\mathit{shared}}
must be satisfied, meaning that
a_{1}
or
a_{2}
must travel in bad weather, or not be at the base often enough.
The four potential value decompositions for
v=\frac{5}{6}
are
(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},1,1)
,
(0,1,1,1)
,
(1,0,1,1)
,
(1,1,0,1)
.
The last tuple is impossible to achieve in the described environment.

(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},1,1)
corresponds to case where both
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
travel in bad weather.
The other two, to either
a_{1}
or
a_{2}
not being at the base for two steps infinitely often.
All three are possible for any
\frac{5}{6}
-hopeful input sequence.
A most permissive
\mathit{ADec}
will thus contain
(\frac{5}{6},\mathsf{Hopeful}(\frac{5}{6}),
\{(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},1,1),
(0,1,1,1),(1,0,1,1)\})
. An alternative is to take a subset, enforcing specific priorities.

While allowed decompositions maps impose requirements on the contribution by each agent to the overall satisfaction value,
they do not specify
how
the agents cooperate to achieve these values.
This is done by providing for each
v\in\mathit{Vals}(\Phi)
an
assume guarantee contract
which specifies what assumptions each agent makes on the behavior of other agents and what guarantees it provides in return.
We recall the definition from
(
Dewes and Dimitrova 2023
)
.

Definition 3
.

An
assume-guarantee contract
for
n
agents is a tuple
\langle(A_{a},G_{a})\rangle_{a=1}^{n}
, where for every agent
a\in\mathsf{Agt}
:

•

A_{a},G_{a}\subseteq(2^{\mathit{AP}})^{\omega}
are safety languages defining the assumption and guarantee, respectively, of agent
a
.

•

\bigcap_{a^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\setminus\{a\}}G_{a^{\prime}}\subseteq A_{a}
.

•

For every finite trace
\sigma\in(2^{\mathit{AP}})^{*}
:

(i)

If there exists an infinite word
\sigma^{\prime}\in(2^{\mathit{AP}})^{\omega}
such that
\sigma\cdot\sigma^{\prime}\in A_{a}
, then, for every
o_{a}\in 2^{\mathit{O}_{a}}
, there exists
\sigma^{\prime\prime}\in(2^{\mathit{AP}})^{\omega}
with
\sigma\cdot\sigma^{\prime\prime}\in A_{a}
and
\mathsf{proj}(\sigma^{\prime\prime}[0],\mathit{O}_{a})=o_{a}
.

(ii)

If there exists an infinite word
\sigma^{\prime}\in(2^{\mathit{AP}})^{\omega}
such that
\sigma\cdot\sigma^{\prime}\in G_{a}
, then, for every
o_{\overline{a}}\in 2^{\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}}
, there exists
\sigma^{\prime\prime}\in(2^{\mathit{AP}})^{\omega}
with
\sigma\cdot\sigma^{\prime\prime}\in G_{a}
and
\mathsf{proj}(\sigma^{\prime\prime}[0],\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}})=o_{\overline{a}}
.

The above conditions ensure two properties.
Agent
a
cannot violate its own assumption
A_{a}
by selecting a bad output
o_{a}
.
The remaining agents cannot violate the guarantee which agent
a
must provide by selecting a bad output
o_{\overline{a}}
.

We are now ready to give the definition of a good-enough decomposition contract, which pairs up an allowed decompositions map with a function mapping each
v\in\mathit{Vals}(\Phi)
to an assume-guarantee contract.
As a result, for each
v\in\mathit{Vals}(\Phi)
, the contract prescribes allowed decompositions, depending on the
v
-hopeful input sequences, and an associated assume-guarantee contract
\langle(A_{a}^{v},G_{a}^{v})\rangle_{a=1}^{n}
.

Definition 4

(Good-Enough Decomposition Contract)
.

Let
\Phi
be a compositional LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
specification over

\mathit{AP}=\mathit{I}\uplus\mathit{O}
, where

\mathit{O}=\mathit{O}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{O}_{n}
is a partitioning of
O

among
n
agents.
Let

\mathit{ADec}
be a map of allowed decompositions for
\Phi
, and let

\mathit{AG}
be a function that maps each
v\in\mathit{Vals}(\Phi)

to an assume-guarantee contract

\langle(A_{a}^{v},G_{a}^{v})\rangle_{a=1}^{n}
.
We say that
(\mathit{ADec},\mathit{AG})
is a
good-enough decomposition contract
for
\Phi
and
\mathit{O}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{O}_{n}

if and only if
for every
v\in\mathit{Vals}(\Phi)
and

\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\in\mathsf{Hopeful}(v,\Phi)
,
there exist

(v^{\prime},H,D)\in\mathit{ADec}
,
\mathbf{d}\in D
and

\sigma_{\mathit{O}}\in(2^{\mathit{O}})^{\omega}
,
such that
v^{\prime}\geq v
and the following hold:

(i)

\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\in H
,
\llbracket\Psi_{\mathit{shared}},\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\parallel\sigma_{\mathit{O}}\rrbracket=\mathbf{d}[s]
,

(ii)

\llbracket\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{a},\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\parallel\sigma_{\mathit{O}}\rrbracket=\mathbf{d}[a]
for all
a\in[n]
, and

(iii)

(\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\parallel\sigma_{\mathit{O}})\in\bigcap_{a\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}A_{a}^{v^{\prime}}
.

The conditions in
Definition

4
ensure compatibility between
\mathit{ADec}
and
\mathit{AG}
.
More concretely, for each
v
-hopeful input sequence
\sigma_{\mathit{I}}
there exists a corresponding output trace that agrees with some allowed value decomposition for value
v^{\prime}\geq v
,
and, in addition is consistent with the assumptions of all agents associated with value
v^{\prime}
.
The next example illustrates the importance of these conditions.

Example 4.2
.

A GEDC for
\Phi
in
Example

1.1
combines some
\mathit{ADec}
with an assume-guarantee contract to capture the coordination between the agents.
First, in input sequences where
\mathsf{call}
is true at least once, each agent must rely on help from other agents to cover
\Psi_{\mathit{shared}}
. More concretely, agents
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
assume that
a_{3}
will always respond.

When the best possible value is
v=\frac{5}{6}
,

a_{1}
and
a_{2}
must coordinate whether one of them should respond to all calls,
or if both will reduce their local satisfaction value to
\frac{1}{2}
.

This is reflected in
\mathit{ADec}
and the assume-guarantee contract.
If agent
a_{1}
guarantees to cover all calls in bad weather for some input sequences, and agent
a_{2}
guarantees the same for all of the remaining input sequences, then value decomposition
(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},1,1)
is not attainable, and both
(0,1,1,1)
and
(1,0,1,1)
must be in
\mathit{ADec}
.
Alternatively, if the assumptions and guarantees force
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
to alternate responding to calls, with both potentially travelling in bad weather, then
(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},1,1)
is the only matching value decomposition for value
v=\frac{5}{6}
.

Since a GEDC associates assumptions with each value
v
, it can enforce coordination for worst-case input sequences, while giving the agents more freedom when the environment is more helpful (allows a higher satisfaction value).

Remark.

GEDC are more general than the contracts in
(
Dewes and Dimitrova 2023
)
in two key aspects.
First, GEDC allow us to relate obligations to the hopefulness level of input sequences with respect to the overall specification, instead of being independent of the global combined value.
Second, GEDC include explicit value decompositions which offer a more natural way to quantify the required contribution of each agent, as well as flexibility in specifying obligations.
This results in a complete decomposition rule.

The value decompositions of an allowed decompositions map
\mathit{ADec}
in a GEDC specify obligations for each agent
a
in terms of values for the local and the shared specification which
a
must ensure.
An agent
a
should meet its obligation with respect to the value of
\Psi_{\mathit{shared}}
only if the other agents provide output sequences that permit that, that is, if
behaviors of the other agents are collaborative
.
Otherwise,
a
is expected to achieve the best possible value for
\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{a}
.
We call a sequence
\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}}\in(2^{\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}})^{\omega}

D
-collaborative for input sequence
\sigma_{\mathit{I}}
,
if there exists an output sequence

\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{a}}\in(2^{\mathit{O}_{a}})^{\omega}
for agent
a
and a value decomposition
\mathbf{d}\in D
where

•

\llbracket\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{a},(\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\parallel\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}}\parallel\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{a}})\rrbracket\geq\mathbf{d}[a]
, and

•

\llbracket\Psi_{\mathit{shared}},(\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\parallel\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}}\parallel\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{a}})\rrbracket\geq\mathbf{d}[s]
.

We denote by
\mathsf{Collab}(D,\sigma_{\mathit{I}})_{\bar{a}}
the set of all such sequences.

Intuitively,
D
-collaborative behaviors of
\overline{a}
are those that permit agent
a
to provide output such that the resulting values for
\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{a}
and
\Psi_{\mathit{shared}}
conform to at least one decomposition in
D
.
This is illustrated by the following example.

Example 4.3
.

In the setting of
Example

1.1
, for input sequences where
\mathsf{call}
is always true and
\mathsf{badw}
is always false, a value
v=1
for
\Phi
is possible with
D=\{(1,1,1,1)\}
.
In this case, the
D
-collaborative outputs for
a_{1}
are those where agents
a_{2}
and
a_{3}
take care of
enough
calls such that
a_{1}
can achieve full satisfaction of
\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{1}
.
More specifically, if
a_{2}
sets
\mathsf{at\_site}_{2}
to true every
k
steps, that will give
a_{1}
enough freedom to set
\mathsf{at\_base}_{1}
to true for two consecutive turns every
k
steps, and still meet its obligations towards
\Psi_{\mathit{shared}}
.

We will now state what it means for a MAS
\mathcal{S}
to satisfy a GEDC.
Intuitively, the implementation
M_{a}
of each agent
a
should satisfy certain obligations for each
v\in\mathit{Vals}(\Phi)

and combination of
v
-hopeful input sequence
\sigma_{\mathit{I}}
with outputs of the agents in
\overline{a}
satisfying the assumption
A_{a}^{v}
.
If the outputs of the agents in
\overline{a}
are collaborative for
\sigma_{\mathit{I}}
and
D
, the obligation is determined by the value distributions in
D
, and otherwise, by the best value of
\varphi^{a}_{\mathit{local}}
possible for
\sigma_{\mathit{I}}
.

Definition 5

(GEDC Satisfaction)
.

Let
\Phi
be a compositional LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
specification over
\mathit{AP}=\mathit{I}\uplus\mathit{O}
,

\mathit{O}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{O}_{n}
be a partitioning of
O
,
and let
(\mathit{ADec},\mathit{AG})
be a GEDC for
\Phi
and
\mathit{O}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{O}_{n}
.
We say that a MAS
\mathcal{S}=(\mathit{I},\mathit{O},\mathcal{M})

satisfies a GEDC
(\mathit{ADec},\mathit{AG})
iff
for every agent
a\in\mathsf{Agt}
, its implementation
M_{a}
satisfies the following condition.

For every

\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\in(2^{\mathit{I}})^{\omega}
and every
sequence of outputs of the other agents

\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}}\in(2^{\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}})^{\omega}
,
for every
(v,H,D)\in\mathit{ADec}
with

\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\in H
and

(\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\parallel M_{a}(\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\parallel\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}})\parallel\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}})\in A_{a}^{v}
,
where
\mathit{AG}(v)=\langle(A_{a}^{v},G_{a}^{v})\rangle_{a=1}^{n}
,

all
of the following hold:

(i)

If
\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}}\in\mathsf{Collab}(D,\sigma_{\mathit{I}})_{\bar{a}}
, then for all
\mathbf{d}\in D
,

if there is an output sequence
\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{a}}\in(2^{\mathit{O}_{a}})^{\omega}
, with

•

\llbracket\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{a},(\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\parallel\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{a}}\parallel\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}})\rrbracket\geq\mathbf{d}[a]
, and

•

\llbracket\Psi_{\mathit{shared}},(\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\parallel\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{a}}\parallel\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}})\rrbracket\geq\mathbf{d}[s]
,

then, there exists
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\in D
where

•

\mathbf{d}^{\prime}[a^{\prime}]=\mathbf{d}[a^{\prime}]
for all
a^{\prime}\neq a
, and

•

\llbracket\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{a},(\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\parallel M_{a}(\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\parallel\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}})\parallel\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}})\rrbracket\geq\mathbf{d}^{\prime}[a]
,

•

\llbracket\Psi_{\mathit{shared}},(\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\parallel M_{a}(\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\parallel\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}})\parallel\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}})\rrbracket\geq\mathbf{d}^{\prime}[s]
.

(ii)

If
\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}}\in\mathsf{Collab}(D,\sigma_{I})_{\bar{a}}
, then the guarantee
G_{a}^{v}
is satisfied, that is,

(\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\parallel M_{a}(\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\parallel\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}})\parallel\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}})\in G_{a}^{v}
.

(iii)

If
\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}}\not\in\mathsf{Collab}(D,\sigma_{I})_{\bar{a}}
,
then for
u\in\mathit{Vals}(\varphi^{a}_{\mathit{local}})
,
if it holds that
\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\in\mathsf{Hopeful}(u,\varphi^{a}_{\mathit{local}})
, then it also holds that

\llbracket\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{a},(\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\parallel M_{a}(\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\parallel\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}})\parallel\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}})\rrbracket\geq u
.

Generally, for the contract to be satisfied, the output
M_{a}(\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\parallel\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}})
produced by an agent
a
must
(i)
achieve best-effort satisfaction for
\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{a}
and
\Psi_{\mathit{shared}}
depending on
\sigma_{\mathit{I}}
,
in accordance with the decompositions
\mathbf{d}
contained in
\mathit{ADec}
and
(ii)
satisfy the guarantees
G_{a}^{v}
as specified by the contract
(\mathit{ADec},\mathit{AG})
.
What section of the contract applies primarily depends on which set
H
the sequence
\sigma_{\mathit{I}}
belongs to.
There are two exceptions to the above requirement, depending on the behavior
\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}}
of other agents
\bar{a}
.
First, if some of the assumptions
A_{a}^{v}
is violated, agent
a
is free from any obligations.
Second, as stated by
(iii)
, if
\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}}
is not collaborative, agent
a
must only maximize the satisfaction of
\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{a}
.

The next theorem states that GEDCs are
sound
.
That is, a system that satisfies a GEDC
(\mathit{ADec},\mathit{AG})
for a compositional LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
specification
\Phi
, is guaranteed to satisfy
\Phi
.

Theorem 1

(Soundness of GEDC)
.

Let
\Phi
be a compositional LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
specification and
(\mathit{ADec},\mathit{AG})
be a GEDC for
\Phi
.
Then, for every MAS
\mathcal{S}=(\mathit{I},\mathit{O},\langle M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}\rangle)
it holds that if
\mathcal{S}\models(\mathit{ADec},\mathit{AG})
, then
\mathcal{S}\models\Phi
.

Proof Sketch.

To show that
\mathcal{S}\models\Phi
, we show for every

\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\in\mathsf{Hopeful}(v,\Phi)
that
\llbracket\Phi,\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\parallel\mathcal{M}(\sigma_{\mathit{I}})\rrbracket\geq v
.
To do so, we prove that for some
a\in\mathsf{Agt}
, the outputs of the other agents are
D
-collaborative for
\sigma_{\mathit{I}}
and some
D
, and satisfy the assumption
A_{a}^{v}
. Applying
(i)
in
Definition

5
this yields
\llbracket\Phi,\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\parallel\mathcal{M}(\sigma_{\mathit{I}})\rrbracket\geq v
.
To establish the existence of agent
a
with this property, we use the
fact that
\Psi_{\mathit{shared}}
is a safety LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
formula and that
(iii)
enforces maximizing the satisfaction value of the local specifications for non-collaborative inputs, to show that the converse is impossible.
∎

GEDCs are also complete, i.e., if
\mathcal{S}\models\Phi
, then there exists a GEDC
(\mathit{ADec},\mathit{AG})
for
\Phi
with

\mathcal{S}\models(\mathit{ADec},\mathit{AG})
.

Theorem 2
.

Let
\Phi
be a compositional specification.
If
\mathcal{S}=(\mathit{I},\mathit{O},\langle M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}\rangle)
is a MAS with
\mathcal{S}\models\Phi
,
then there exists a GEDC
(\mathit{ADec},\mathit{AG})
for
\Phi
where
\mathcal{S}\models(\mathit{ADec},\mathit{AG})
.

Proof Sketch.

\mathcal{S}
produces unique outputs for each input sequence, the resulting trace satisfying exactly one value decomposition
\mathbf{d}
.

\mathit{ADec}
is chosen to contain exactly the finitely many value decompositions realized by
\mathcal{S}
. The input traces matching each
\mathbf{d}
form the respective set
H
.
For
\mathit{AG}
, we define each
G_{a}^{v}
as the safety language encoding the implementation
M_{a}
, and the assumption
A_{a}^{v}
as
\bigwedge_{a^{\prime}\neq a}{G^{v}_{a^{\prime}}}
.
Thus,
\mathit{AG}
prescribes the precise implementation
M_{a}
of each agent
a
.
The full proof is in the supplementary material.
∎

5
Compositional Verification

In this section we present an automata-theoretic method for checking contract satisfaction as formalized in
Definition

5
:
Given a compositional LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
specification
\Phi
,
a GEDC
(\mathit{ADec},\mathit{AG})
for
\Phi
,
and a system
\mathcal{S}=(\mathit{I},\mathit{O},\langle M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}\rangle)
,
decide whether
\mathcal{S}\models(\mathit{ADec},\mathit{AG})
.
Further, we show how to check whether a given pair
(\mathit{ADec},\mathit{AG})
is a GEDC for
\Phi
, that is, it satisfies the conditions of
Definition

2
,
Definition

3
and
Definition

4
.

By
Theorem

1
, if
\mathcal{S}\models(\mathit{ADec},\mathit{AG})
, we can conclude that

\mathcal{S}\models\Phi
.
We remark that the specification
\Phi
can also be checked directly, using standard techniques, by constructing the product of
M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}
.
This, however, is often undesirable.
Working with the full specification and the product system hampers scalability.
Furthermore, a GEDC allows for the independent re-implementation of the individual agents as long as the conditions in
Definition

5
are satisfied.

Procedure for Checking Contract Satisfaction

We first recall necessary automata definitions and constructions.

A
generalized nondeterministic Büchi automaton
(GNBA) over an alphabet

\Sigma
is a tuple
\mathcal{A}=(\Sigma,Q,\delta,Q_{0},\alpha)
, where

Q
is a finite set of states,
Q_{0}\subseteq Q
is a set of
initial states,
\delta:Q\times\Sigma\to 2^{Q}
is the transition function,
and
\alpha\subseteq 2^{Q}
a set of sets of accepting states.
A run
\rho
of a GNBA is accepting iff for every
F\in\alpha
,

\rho
visits
F
infinitely often.
A word
\sigma\in\Sigma^{\omega}
is accepted by
\mathcal{A}
if there exists an accepting run of
\mathcal{A}
on
\sigma
.
The language of
\mathcal{A}
is
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}):=\{\sigma\in\Sigma^{\omega}\mid\sigma\text{ is accepted by }\mathcal{A}\}
.

In
(
Almagor, Boker, and Kupferman 2016
)
it is shown that for any LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
formula
\varphi
over
\mathit{AP}
, and

V\subseteq\mathit{Vals}(\varphi)
,
there exists a GNBA
\mathcal{A}_{\varphi,V}
such that

\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{\varphi,V})
consists of the words
\sigma\in(2^{\mathit{AP}})^{\omega}
with
\llbracket\varphi,\sigma\rrbracket\in V
, and

\mathcal{A}_{\varphi,V}
has at most
2^{(|\varphi|^{2})}
states and at most
|\varphi|
sets of accepting states.
We employ this construction to reduce the problem of checking contract satisfaction to checking emptiness of GNBA.

For the remainder of this section we assume that
(\mathit{ADec},\mathit{AG})
is finitely represented using
\omega
-automata.
More concretely, the set
H
in each
(v,H,D)\in\mathit{ADec}
is represented by a GNBA
\mathcal{H}
where
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})=\{\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\parallel\sigma_{\mathit{O}}\in(2^{\mathit{AP}})^{\omega}\mid\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\in H\}
.
For each
v\in\mathit{Vals}(\Phi)
, the assume-guarantee contract

\mathit{AG}(v)=\langle(A_{a}^{v},G_{a}^{v})\rangle_{a=1}^{n}
is represented by tuple of pairs of GNBA with alphabet
2^{\mathit{AP}}
, one for the assumption language
A_{a}^{v}
, and one for the complement
2^{\mathit{AP}}\setminus G_{a}^{v}
of the guarantee. We denote these automata by

\mathcal{A}_{a}^{v}
and

\overline{\mathcal{G}_{a}^{v}}
.
We assume that all automata in the contract are given as nondeterministic Büchi automata, i.e., have one accepting set.

In the next proposition we outline the construction of a GNBA
\mathcal{C}_{a}
for agent
a
, whose language consists of the traces that violate some of the conditions in
Definition

5
.
The full construction is given in the supplementary material.

Proposition 1
.

For each agent
a\in\mathsf{Agt}
, we can construct a GNBA
\mathcal{C}_{a}
such that for any
\sigma=\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\parallel\sigma_{\mathit{O}}\in(2^{\mathit{AP}})^{\omega}
we have that

\sigma\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{C}_{a})
if and only if there exists
(v,H,D)\in\mathit{ADec}
such that

\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\in H
, and
\sigma\in A_{a}^{v}
, and at least one of the conditions in
Definition

5
is violated for agent
a
.

Proof Sketch.

From the formulas
\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{a}
and
\Psi_{\mathit{shared}}
,
we construct for

\sim\;\in\{<,\geq,=\}
,

u\in\mathit{Vals}(\varphi^{a}_{\mathit{local}})
and

w\in\mathit{Vals}(\Psi_{\mathit{shared}})

the GNBA

\mathcal{B}_{a}^{\sim u}
, and
\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\sim w}
such that

\sigma\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B}_{a}^{\sim u})
iff
\llbracket\varphi^{a}_{\mathit{local}},\sigma\rrbracket\sim u
, and similarly for
\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\sim w}
.

Next, we construct a GNBA
\mathcal{C}_{a}
that characterizes the language of traces
\sigma=\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\parallel\sigma_{\mathit{O}_{a}}\parallel\sigma_{{\mathit{O}_{\overline{a}}}}
such that there exists
(v,H,D)\in\mathit{ADec}

with
\sigma_{\mathit{I}}\in H
, and
\sigma\in A_{a}^{v}
, and some of the conditions
(i)
,
(ii)
or
(iii)
in
Definition

5
is violated.

\mathcal{C}_{a}
is constructed from
\mathcal{B}_{a}^{\sim u},\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\sim w},\mathcal{A}_{a}^{v},\overline{\mathcal{G}_{a}^{v}}
and
\mathcal{H}
using standard automata operations on GNBA.
∎

Using the automata
\mathcal{C}_{a}
constructed in
Proposition

1
, we can automatically check whether
\mathcal{S}\models(\mathit{ADec},\mathit{AG})
by checking
for each agent
a\in\mathsf{Agt}
,
whether the intersection of the languages
of
\mathcal{C}_{a}
and the
implementation
M_{a}
is empty.
If
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{C}_{a})\cap\mathcal{L}(M_{a})=\emptyset
,
then
M_{a}
satisfies conditions of
Definition

5
, otherwise we find a counterexample.
If for all
a\in\mathsf{Agt}
we establish

\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{C}_{a})\cap\mathcal{L}(M_{a})=\emptyset
, we conclude that
\mathcal{S}\models(\mathit{ADec},\mathit{AG})
, and hence
\mathcal{S}\models\Phi
.
Otherwise, we have that
\mathcal{S}\not\models(\mathit{ADec},\mathit{AG})
.
This, however, does not imply
\mathcal{S}\not\models\Phi
, since the GEDC might be insufficient to verify
\Phi
.

Remark.

When checking whether
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{C}_{a})\cap\mathcal{L}(M_{a})=\emptyset
, this can be done separately for each value
v\in Vals(\Phi)
, and incrementally when constructing
\mathcal{C}_{a}
.
We further check emptiness on automata intersections in an on-the-fly manner.

The number of states of the GNBA
\mathcal{C}_{a}
is at most

2^{2^{\mathcal{O}(|\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{a}|^{2}+|\Psi_{\mathit{shared}}|^{2})}}\cdot\sum_{(v,\mathcal{H},D)\in\mathit{ADec}}|\mathcal{H}|\cdot|\mathcal{A}_{a}^{v}|\cdot|\overline{\mathcal{G}_{a}}^{v}|.

Checking contract satisfaction is done by checking for each agent the emptiness of the intersection of
\mathcal{C}_{a}
and the respective implementation
M_{a}
, which can be done in time linear in the size of their product
(
Baier and Katoen 2008
)
.

Theorem 3

(Checking GEDC Satisfaction)
.

Checking if a MAS
\mathcal{S}=(\mathit{I},\mathit{O},\langle M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}\rangle)

satisfies a GEDC
(\mathit{ADec},\mathit{AG})
for a compositional LTL
[\mathcal{F}]
specification
\Phi
can be done for each agent
a\in\mathsf{Agt}
in time linear in

|M_{a}|\cdot 2^{2^{\mathcal{O}(|\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{a}|^{2}+|\Psi_{\mathit{shared}}|^{2})}}\cdot\sum_{(v,\mathcal{H},D)\in\mathit{ADec}}|\mathcal{H}|\cdot|\mathcal{A}_{a}^{v}|\cdot|\overline{\mathcal{G}_{a}}^{v}|
.

Procedure for Checking
(\mathit{ADec},\mathit{AG})
is a GEDC for
\Phi

To check that
(\mathit{ADec},\mathit{AG})
is a GEDC for
\Phi
, we construct the automaton for
\Phi
, of size at most
2^{(|\Phi|^{2})}
.
The conditions are established by checking language inclusion and emptiness for GNBA obtained from those for
\Phi
and
(\mathit{ADec},\mathit{AG})
.
Both can be done in time exponential in the size of the resulting automata, resulting in a procedure that
for each entry
(v,H,D)\in\mathit{ADec}
runs in time exponential in the size of the automata
\mathcal{H}
,
\mathcal{A}_{a}^{v}
and
\overline{\mathcal{G}_{a}}^{v}
and double exponential in
|\Phi|^{2}
.

Modular Verification

The contract-based approach allows for modular verification and design.
In a MAS that satisfies a given GEDC, the local modification of an agent’s implementation or specification can be analyzed without considering the full specification or implementation.

Consider a compositional specification
\Phi
,
a GEDC
(\mathit{ADec},\mathit{AG})
for
\Phi
,
and a MAS
\mathcal{S}=(\mathit{I},\mathit{O},\langle M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}\rangle)
such that
\mathcal{S}\models(\mathit{ADec},\mathit{AG})
.
Suppose that the local specification for agent
a
is modified from
{\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{a}}
to
\widehat{\varphi}_{\mathit{local}}^{a}
, resulting in a revised global specification
\widehat{\Phi}
.
Let
\widehat{M}_{a}
be a new implementation of agent
a
, and

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}=(\mathit{I},\mathit{O},\langle M_{1},\ldots\widehat{M}_{a},\ldots,M_{n}\rangle)
the resulting MAS.
In order to check whether
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}\models(\mathit{ADec},\mathit{AG})
, it suffices to verify only the condition in
Definition

5
pertaining to agent
a
, with respect to the new implementation
\widehat{M}_{a}
of agent
a
and the original local specification
\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{a}
.
If this is the case, by
Theorem

1
, we can conclude that the modified system
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
satisfies the original specification
\Phi
.
Otherwise, the new implementation
\widehat{M}_{a}
of agent
a
is incompatible with the given contract.

Considering the new local specification
\widehat{\varphi}_{\mathit{local}}^{a}
, if
\widehat{M}_{a}
satisfies the condition in
Definition

5
with respect to
\widehat{\varphi}_{\mathit{local}}^{a}
, we can still conclude that
\widehat{M}_{a}
satisfies agent
a
’s obligation towards the remaining agents, but not necessarily
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}\models\widehat{\Phi}
.
The reason is that if
\widehat{\varphi}_{\mathit{local}}^{a}
imposes weaker constraints on the coordination between agents than
\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{a}
, the old GEDC
(\mathit{ADec},\mathit{AG})
might be overly constraining and the overall system sub-optimal.
This is illustrated in the next example.

Example 5.1
.

If the local requirements for an agent are tightened in the design process,
the agent’s new specification might become incompatible with the existing contract, and thus require revision of the contract and the implementations of the other agents.
If, on the other hand, the designer relaxes the local requirements, the satisfaction of the existing contract becomes easier.
However, the contract might not be a GEDC for the new specification
\widehat{\Phi}
,
and the existing implementation of the MAS might no longer be optimal with respect to
\widehat{\Phi}
.
This is because in contrast to traditional requirements, where weaker requirements are always satisfied by a stricter implementation, here we impose an optimality criterion.
To see this, suppose that in the setting of
Example

1.1
, the local specification for agent
a_{1}
no longer reduces in value if the vehicle travels in bad weather.
That is,
\widehat{\varphi}_{\mathit{local}}^{1}
is obtained from
\varphi_{\mathit{local}}^{1}
by dropping the last conjunct. Regardless of the given GEDC, any implementation for agent
a_{1}
that conforms to the original contract clearly still satisfies the conditions of
Definition

5
with respect to
\widehat{\varphi}_{\mathit{local}}^{1}
.
However, if the given contract requires agent
a_{2}
to travel during bad weather, the current implementation does not good-enough satisfy the new specification
\widehat{\Phi}
and the contract is not a GEDC for
\widehat{\Phi}
.
The reason is that
{\widehat{\varphi}_{\mathit{local}}^{1}}
allows vehicle
a_{1}
to travel in bad weather and still achieve local satisfaction of
1
, making implementations where vehicle
a_{2}
travels in bad weather sub-optimal.
Intuitively, relaxing the local specification for
a_{1}
enables the satisfaction of
\varphi^{2}_{\mathit{local}}
with a higher value than stipulated by the GEDC for
\Phi
, resulting in a higher possible satisfaction value for the new specification
\widehat{\Phi}
.
As the old contract permits implementations that are not good-enough with respect to
\widehat{\Phi}
, it needs to be revised.

6
Experimental Evaluation and Conclusion

To evaluate our framework, we implemented the proposed compositional verification procedure in a prototype in Python using the Spot automata library
(
Duret-Lutz et al. 2022
)
.
We ran experiments on 6 MASs each with a number of agents between 3 and 6.
The results, obtained on a consumer laptop with 16 GB of memory, are shown in
Table

1
.
The benchmarks are provided in the supplementary material.

Example
